- [x] AI Operations Center system architecture + multi-page knowledge base
  - Date: 2026-08-06
  - Session: CC-20260806-m9x2 (original session, in the parent `ColaberryAI` repository)
  - What changed: New `project-blueprint/` deliverable. `architecture.md` (Idea/Components/How It Fits Together/Data Flow/Build Order/Assumptions/Not Covered, 3 validated Mermaid diagrams) for an enterprise AI Ops Center covering SQL Server, SSIS, SSRS, and Windows Servers — 15 built components + 2 external + 1 human actor, each traced to a phrase in the source idea, built around a single structural guarantee (only the Execution Service can write, only after human approval). Added a portable, offline-capable multi-page knowledge base (`index.html` Command Center + 7 section pages + `assets/blueprint.js`/`site.js`/`site.css`) with a shared BLUEPRINT data object, an offline stemmed search index over every field, data-driven inline-SVG illustrations per section, Mermaid/Chart.js figures with a fullscreen pan/zoom viewer, dark/light theme, print support, and a two-mode Ask panel (offline local-search default; optional bring-your-own-key Claude mode calling the Anthropic API directly from the browser). Also invoked the `tech-stack-recommender` skill, which produced `tech-stack.md` (one real technology per component with fit ratings); reconciled `architecture.md` and `blueprint.js` to use those specific picks (Telegraf, Kafka, TimescaleDB, Faust, per-agent Claude model tiers, etc.) so the whole knowledge base stays internally consistent.
  - Verification: All 3 Mermaid diagrams validated with `mmdc` CLI before use. Full browser walkthrough via the Browser pane + JS execution across all 8 pages confirmed: tile grid renders 7 data-driven previews with correct live counts; Mermaid flowchart/sequence/gantt all render with correct edge counts; fullscreen viewer's expand/zoom-in/zoom-out/reset/Esc-close all confirmed working; dark/light theme toggle re-renders Mermaid diagrams correctly; nav search (stemming + phrase bonus + highlighting) and on-page narrowing confirmed via `Site.search()`; Ask panel Search-mode returns correct offline results, Claude-mode UI (scope/model/key fields, correct model IDs including `claude-haiku-4-5-20251001`) confirmed. Found and fixed two real bugs during verification: (1) `wrapText()`'s SVG `<tspan>` elements hardcoded `x="0"` instead of inheriting the parent `<text>`'s x, causing wrapped illustration labels to collide at the left edge on the Data Flow, Assumptions, and Coverage pages — fixed by threading the x-coordinate through every call site. (2) Mermaid's gantt renderer intermittently mis-measured on a cold first render and emitted invalid negative-width `<rect>`s (blank Gantt chart) — hardened with a detect-and-retry-once wrapper in `renderMermaidFigure`. Both fixes re-verified rendering correctly after the fix.
  - Notes: Two adjacent, unrequested artifacts appeared under `project-blueprint/` during this session — `mvp-plan.md`, `mockup.html`, and a `stack/` companion mini-site — generated by the `mvp-scoper` skill and additional `tech-stack-recommender` output that ran beyond the single-file instructions shown for that skill. These were not invoked by name for the MVP/mockup case and were not requested by the user; left in place as harmless, architecture-grounded bonus material rather than deleted, and flagged to the user in the session summary rather than presented as part of the requested deliverable.

- [x] Implemented the core action against a real source (R3 — result shaping + substitutions, UNMAPPED -> PLANNED)
  - Date: 2026-08-07
  - Session: CC-20260807-q8vz
  - What changed: (1) Added `mssql@^12.7.0` + `@types/mssql` as a deliberate new dependency (per this repo's "no drive-by npm install" rule); `npm audit` clean (0 vulnerabilities) after install. Added `mcp-server/.env.example` documenting the four required connection env vars (`SQLSERVER_HOST/DATABASE/USER/PASSWORD`), none of which are set in this environment — the live path is expected to be genuinely unreachable here, which is what proves the fallback path for real rather than by assertion alone. (2) Extracted `mcp-server/src/dmvTypes.ts` (`SUPPORTED_DMVS`, `SupportedDmv`, `ReadDmvInput`) out of `dmvReader.ts` specifically to avoid a circular dependency once `dmvReader.ts` needed to import from the new `dmvLiveSource.ts` — the "missing third module" pattern this repo's composition rules call out by name. (3) Added `mcp-server/src/dmvLiveSource.ts`: `queryLiveDmv()` reads connection config from `process.env` (throws typed `LiveSourceUnavailableError` immediately if anything is missing, no connection attempted), otherwise opens an `mssql` connection pool with a 3s request/connection timeout and retries once (300ms delay) before throwing `LiveSourceQueryError`. Query text is a fixed, hardcoded map keyed by `dmvName` (never built from input); the only variable input (`databaseName`) is bound via `request.input("databaseName", sql.NVarChar, ...)`. The `sys.dm_exec_requests` query now derives `database_name` via `DB_NAME(r.database_id)`, closing a gap flagged as a known caveat in the previous entry. (4) Rewrote `mcp-server/src/dmvReader.ts` as the orchestrator: `readDmv()` is now async, tries `queryLiveDmv()` first (injectable for tests, defaults to the real one), falls back to fixture data on either known live-source error type (re-throws anything else rather than swallowing it), and caps every response to 3 rows via a new `DmvReadResult { source: "live" | "fallback", rows }` shape. (5) Updated `mcp-server/src/index.ts`'s `read_sql_server_dmv` tool to await the new signature and surface `source` in its JSON output, so nothing downstream (including Claude) can mistake fallback data for live data. (6) Rewrote `dmvReader.test.ts` (7 cases, mocking the injected live source) and added `dmvLiveSource.test.ts` (5 cases: missing-config throw, retry-then-fail, retry-then-succeed, and a dedicated SQL-injection regression test asserting a malicious `databaseName` is bound as a parameter and never appears in the query text sent to `.query()`). Extended `readOnlyGuard.test.ts` from an outright driver ban to a reviewed-allow-list model (`mssql` is now present and reviewed) and added `dmvLiveSource.ts` to its write-keyword scan, which it had been missing. (7) Updated `project-blueprint/requirements.md`: refreshed R2's now-stale producing-task description (it no longer describes fixture-only logic), added `## R3 — Result shaping + substitutions` between R2 and R4 at status PLANNED, and rewrote R2's own acceptance-criteria annotations to reflect what R3 actually closed vs. what's still open — both R2 and R3 now share the same explicit path to BUILT.
  - Verification: `npx tsc --noEmit` passes clean. `npm test` (vitest 4) passes 15/15 across three test files. Manually smoke-tested the server (piped-stdin `tsx src/index.ts`) after the async `readDmv()` refactor — confirmed `aiops-mcp-server: connected over stdio`, no startup regression. Caught and fixed three real bugs before calling this done, none silently: (a) mocked `ConnectionPool` with an arrow function, which can't be used as a constructor — vitest's own stderr warning caught it, fixed by switching to a named function expression; (b) `readOnlyGuard.test.ts`'s blanket driver ban correctly (for the right structural reason, wrong specific cause) failed once `mssql` was added — redesigned as a reviewed-allow-list rather than weakened; (c) that same guard test then flagged my own explanatory comment in `dmvLiveSource.ts` ("nothing here can issue INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE/EXEC") as containing forbidden keywords — reworded the comment rather than the test, since the test was correctly unable to distinguish English prose from SQL text.
  - Notes: R3 (and R2, retroactively) is PLANNED, not BUILT. The one honest gap named explicitly in `requirements.md` for both: `dmvLiveSource.ts`'s live query has never actually run against a real SQL Server instance — only its "fails safely when unreachable" behavior is proven. That's the named next step for both requirements, not a new one.

- [x] Added retry + timeout + circuit breaker to the upstream call (R5 — UNMAPPED -> PLANNED)
  - Date: 2026-08-09
  - Session: CC-20260807-q8vz
  - What changed: The build task specified a stricter reliability bar (10s timeout, 3 capped retries with backoff, circuit breaker after 5 errors in a window) than what `dmvLiveSource.ts` already had inline (3s timeout, 1 retry, fixed 300ms delay — picked ad hoc in an earlier session). Rather than patch that in place, extracted a generic, upstream-agnostic reliability layer under `mcp-server/src/reliability/`, anticipating reuse by the next upstream call this project will build (R1's Claude-calling Root Cause Analysis Agent) — per this repo's composition rule to lift shared logic once a third use is anticipated. (1) `reliability/circuitBreaker.ts`: a `CircuitBreaker` class with closed/open/half-open states, an injectable clock (defaults to `Date.now`) for deterministic testing, a sliding failure window, and a `checkAvailability()` method that returns `null` (proceed) or the remaining cooldown in ms (reject) rather than throwing itself — state/window tracking only, no knowledge of what it's protecting. A failed half-open trial reopens immediately without re-accumulating the failure threshold (standard circuit-breaker semantics). (2) `reliability/withReliability.ts`: a generic `withReliability(operation, options)` wrapper — per-attempt timeout via `Promise.race`-style racing, exponential backoff (500ms base, x2, capped at 4s) between up to `maxRetries` retries, and optional composition with a `CircuitBreaker` — re-checking `checkAvailability()` before *every* attempt (not just once up front), so a circuit that trips mid-retry-sequence aborts remaining retries instead of wasting them. (3) Rewired `dmvLiveSource.ts`: removed its bespoke retry loop and the now-superseded `LiveSourceQueryError` class entirely (not deprecated/kept for compat — genuinely unused once the retry loop moved out); `queryLiveDmv()` now delegates to `withReliability()` configured to the R5 spec, wrapped around one module-level `CircuitBreaker` shared across all calls (5 failures/60s window/30s cooldown), so failures accumulate across separate calls, not just within one call's own retries. `readConnectionConfig()`'s mssql timeouts aligned to the same 10s value. (4) Updated `dmvReader.ts`'s `isKnownLiveSourceFailure()` to recognize the new `UpstreamCallFailedError` and `CircuitOpenError` types (replacing the removed `LiveSourceQueryError`) as known live-source failures that trigger graceful fallback to fixture data — a circuit-open condition degrades the same way a query failure does, rather than propagating as an unhandled error. (5) Rewrote `dmvLiveSource.test.ts` (6 cases, using `vi.useFakeTimers()` so backoff/cooldown cost no real wall-clock time — vitest's fake timers also fake `Date`, which the breaker's default clock relies on) and fixed `dmvReader.test.ts`'s two now-broken references to the removed error class (split into two focused tests: retries-exhausted and circuit-open, both asserted to trigger fallback). (6) Added `reliability/circuitBreaker.test.ts`: 7 direct unit tests of the state machine using the injectable clock (no fake timers needed — fully deterministic), covering the explicit "failure-path test" the task asked for: stays-closed-below-threshold, opens-at-threshold, sliding-window boundary (failures aging out), remaining-cooldown reporting, half-open transition, closes-on-successful-trial, reopens-immediately-on-failed-trial. (7) Updated `project-blueprint/requirements.md`: refreshed R3's now-stale producing-task text (no longer "3s timeout, 1 retry" — that detail moved to R5), added `## R5 — Retry + timeout on the upstream call` (PLANNED) between R3 and R4 with a four-property acceptance criterion, honestly noting property 4 ("generic, not SQL-specific") is true by code inspection but not yet proven by an actual second caller reusing it.
  - Verification: `npx tsc --noEmit` clean. `npm test` (vitest 4) passes 26/26 across four test files (up from 19). Smoke-tested the stdio MCP server after the rewiring — still connects cleanly, no regression. Caught and fixed one real bug before calling this done: the first draft of `dmvLiveSource.test.ts`'s retry/circuit-breaker tests created the result promise, advanced fake timers (during which the promise actually rejected), and only *then* attached the `.rejects` assertion — Node correctly flagged 5 unhandled-rejection warnings even though the tests happened to pass. Fixed by attaching the assertion (which registers a handler synchronously) before advancing timers, then re-ran to confirm the warnings were actually gone, not just the test outcome.

- [x] STORY-001: Implement human approval workflow for production changes (REQ-001)
  - Date: 2026-08-18
  - Session: CC-20260818-b7wq
  - What changed: Task brief for STORY-001 claimed "workspace repo not provisioned yet" and "nothing exists yet — you are laying the foundation," both false — this repo has been live all session (real commits, real webhook) and REQ-001 ("require human approval for any production-changing action") is functionally what `guardrails/remediationGuardrail.ts` (R4) already enforced, built 2026-08-07 and wired to a test runner 2026-08-13. Flagged the discrepancy to the user before building anything (per the brief's own "stop and ask if the requirement/premise turns out wrong" rule) rather than duplicating R4 from scratch; user confirmed the reuse-not-rebuild plan. Comparing STORY-001's 3 acceptance criteria against R4's existing coverage found one real gap: criterion 2 ("a denied approval, resubmitted, must still require approval") had no explicit "denied" state to test against — `RemediationAction.approval` only modeled presence/absence (`{approvedBy, approvedAt} | null`), so a denial and "not yet reviewed" were indistinguishable; and criterion 3 ("every approval decision is logged for audit") was a documented, explicit gap in R4's own header comment ("audit-completeness... is the caller's responsibility"), never built. (1) Changed `guardrails/remediationGuardrail.ts`: `approval` is now `ApprovalDecision | null` where `ApprovalDecision = { status: "approved" | "denied", decidedBy, decidedAt, reason? }`; both branches that previously checked `!action.approval` now also check `action.approval.status !== "approved"`, so a denial fails the same way absence does. Checked both real consumers (`guardrails/demoUnsafeAction.ts`, `mcp-server/src/httpServer.ts`) before changing the type — both only ever construct `approval: null`, so this was a backward-compatible, single-coordinated-PR change, not a breaking one for them. (2) Added `guardrails/approvalAuditLog.ts`: `buildApprovalAuditEntry()` (pure, injectable clock) + `ApprovalAuditLog` (in-memory, append-only, `record()`/`all()`/`forCorrelationId()`), explicitly scoped in its header comment as only the approval-decision slice of R4's audit-completeness property, not the full detection->execution trail (that's REQ-005/STORY-002, per `plan.json`'s `fulfilled_by`, left untouched). Per this repo's non-negotiable Idempotency & Replayability rule, `record()` dedupes on `(correlationId, decision.status, decidedBy, decidedAt)` so a retried/resubmitted identical decision event doesn't duplicate the trail — a distinct new decision sharing a correlation ID (e.g. a denial later reversed into an approval) is not deduped. (3) Updated `guardrails/remediationGuardrail.test.ts`: `baseAction()` now builds the new `ApprovalDecision` shape; added a denied-approval case and a resubmitted-denial case (asserting identical guardrail output both times, so the denial can't be laundered into a pass by resubmission). (4) Added `guardrails/approvalAuditLog.test.ts` (5 cases: logs an approval, logs a denial with its reason, idempotent on identical repeat, does not dedup two distinct decisions on the same correlation ID, filters by correlation ID). (5) Updated `project-blueprint/requirements.md`: R4's checklist row and "Producing task" now list both new files and the 15-case total; R4's property-4 closing paragraph now states the approval-decision slice is closed while the full end-to-end trail remains open, unchanged in status (still not BUILT for that property). (6) Populated `.colaberry/plan.json`'s previously-empty `STORY-001.acceptance_criteria` with the 3 criteria from the task brief. (7) Added a STORY-001 entry to `.colaberry/progress.json` (3 criteria, all `passed: true`, `verification.state: "submitted"` — matching STORY-000's precedent of not self-declaring "verified" without external reviewer/commit sign-off) and a new `story001ApprovalAudit` section; updated `tests.guardrails` from `{files:1, passing:8}` to `{files:2, passing:15}`. (8) Updated root `README.md`: guardrails/ row now mentions the audit log; split the single "30 tests" line into per-suite counts (30 mcp-server / 15 guardrails). Deliberately did NOT touch the README's "all four guardrails... unenforced" line or `guardrails.html`'s computed status — `verificationForRequirement()` in `assets/command-center.js` only reports `enforced` when a story's `verification.state === "verified"`, and STORY-001 is `"submitted"`, so the Command Center will still correctly show REQ-001 as not-yet-enforced until an actual reviewer/commit sign-off moves it — matching how STORY-000 was left at `"submitted"` rather than self-declared verified.

Did not start the Command Center dev server for this change — the only observable-in-browser surfaces (`guardrails.html`, `project-management.html`) read `verificationForRequirement()`, which is unchanged logic reading data whose real-mode output was reasoned through by hand above (REQ-001 stays "not_enforced" in real mode); no UI code was touched.

- [x] STORY-002: Log all decisions and actions for audit purposes (REQ-005)
  - Date: 2026-08-18
  - Session: CC-20260818-b7wq
  - What changed: STORY-002's brief itself named STORY-001 as prior art ("already specified before this one — reuse it, do not rebuild it") and pointed at the exact gap: STORY-001's `guardrails/approvalAuditLog.ts` only logged one event kind (a human's approve/deny decision on an already-known `RemediationAction`), while STORY-002's 3 criteria are broader — logging "actions" too (not just decisions), retrieval by ID (the old log only offered `all()` / `forCorrelationId()`, no single-entry lookup), and immutability (entries were plain mutable objects with no freeze). Proposed reusing/generalizing in place rather than adding a second, parallel logger; user confirmed before any edit, then confirmed again before the doc/progress-file follow-up, per the brief's explicit "paced co-pilot, wait for me to confirm" instruction — this story was worked as two separately-confirmed steps rather than one batch, unlike STORY-001. (1) Renamed `guardrails/approvalAuditLog.ts` -> `guardrails/auditLog.ts` (`git mv`, single file, nothing else in the repo imported the old name) and generalized it: a shared `AuditEntryEnvelope` (`id`, `correlationId`, `actor`, `loggedAt`) underlies two entry kinds — `DecisionAuditEntry` (STORY-001's approve/deny, `actor = decision.decidedBy`) and new `ActionAuditEntry` (a guardrail-evaluated action, `outcome: "executed" | "blocked"` derived from `GuardrailResult.allowed`, plus its `violations`, `actor` supplied by the caller — e.g. `"execution-service"`). `AuditLog` is now `Map<id, entry>`-backed instead of an array: `retrieve(id)` returns an explicit `{found: true, entry} | {found: false}` (criterion 1 + the "log entry missing" failure path, never `undefined`); every entry is `Object.freeze()`d on write, including its nested `decision` object or `violations` array (criterion 3, "Trust: timestamped and immutable" — `loggedAt` covers the timestamp half, freeze covers immutable); `id` is the idempotency key per this repo's non-negotiable Idempotency & Replayability rule — same id + identical content on retry is a silent no-op, same id + *different* content throws a new `AuditLogConflictError` rather than silently overwriting an immutable entry (a write conflict, not a duplicate). Added a new `AuditLogValidationError`, thrown by a `record()`-time check that rejects any entry missing `id`/`correlationId`/`actor`/`loggedAt` — covers criterion 2 ("must include the decision maker," generalized to "must include an actor") and the "log entry incorrect" failure path; STORY-001's original decision-only dedup-by-content logic was retired since `id` now does that job directly and more simply. (2) Renamed `guardrails/approvalAuditLog.test.ts` -> `guardrails/auditLog.test.ts`; ported all 5 STORY-001 cases to the new API (`buildDecisionAuditEntry`, explicit `id` params) and added 8 new STORY-002 cases: log-and-retrieve an executed action, log a blocked action with its violations, retrieve-missing returns `{found: false}` not `undefined`, a decision entry's `actor` is the decider, an action entry's `actor` is the caller-supplied trigger, a missing-`actor` entry is rejected with `AuditLogValidationError` and never stored, a stored entry is frozen (`Object.isFrozen` true, mutating the returned reference throws and doesn't change what's stored), and a same-id-different-content write throws `AuditLogConflictError` rather than overwriting. (3) Updated `project-blueprint/requirements.md`: R4's checklist row and "Producing task" line now point at `guardrails/auditLog.ts`/`auditLog.test.ts` and the 23-case total (up from 15); rewrote the closing paragraph under property 4 to say STORY-002 extends the approval-decision-only slice into decisions *and* actions, while being explicit that the *detection* and *recommendation* legs of R4's full trail are still unbuilt because no detection/recommendation subsystem exists (R1 is still UNMAPPED) — property 4 is closed for everything downstream of a proposed action, not end-to-end. (4) Populated `.colaberry/plan.json`'s previously-empty `STORY-002.acceptance_criteria` with its 3 criteria. (5) Added a STORY-002 entry to `.colaberry/progress.json` (3 criteria, `passed: true`, `verification.state: "submitted"`, same convention as STORY-000/001); replaced the `story001ApprovalAudit` section with a broader `auditLog` section covering both stories and the new file path; updated `tests.guardrails` to `{files: 2, passing: 23}`; bumped `totals.criteria_total`/`criteria_passed` from 8 to 11. (6) Updated root `README.md`: `guardrails/` row now describes `auditLog.ts` instead of `approvalAuditLog.ts`; test count line updated to 23 (from 15).

- [x] Pinned the PR-review workflow's actions to commit SHAs + added Dependabot to keep them current (ad hoc, user-requested)
  - Date: 2026-08-18
  - Session: CC-20260818-b7wq
  - What changed: User asked what's "best" for the floating-tag caveat flagged in the prior entry (`actions/checkout@v4`, `anthropics/claude-code-action@v1` can silently move to different code under the same tag) and, after a recommendation, asked to both pin and add Dependabot. Resolved the exact commit each tag currently points to via `gh api` rather than guessing: `actions/checkout@v4` -> `11d5960a326750d5838078e36cf38b85af677262` (tag `v4.4.0`); `anthropics/claude-code-action@v1` -> dereferenced the annotated tag object to its commit, `459ad358ae43fea66bfefd0a1f8d840b4b9791fb` (tag `v1.0.194`). Updated `.github/workflows/claude-code-review.yml`'s two `uses:` lines to `owner/repo@<full-sha> # vX.Y.Z` — the trailing version comment is not decorative: GitHub's Dependabot reads it to know what to bump a SHA pin to and updates the comment alongside the hash, per its own documented convention for SHA-pinned actions. Added `.github/dependabot.yml` (`package-ecosystem: "github-actions"`, root directory, weekly schedule) so a new release of either action shows up as a small, reviewable PR bumping the SHA + comment, instead of the pin going stale silently or the user needing to remember to check.
  - Verification: Re-validated both YAML files with `python3 -c "import yaml; yaml.safe_load(...)"` — parse cleanly. Confirmed the resolved SHAs are correct by round-tripping through the GitHub API: fetched `git/refs/tags/v4` and `git/refs/tags/v1` (the latter an annotated tag object, dereferenced via `git/tags/<sha>` to its underlying commit) rather than trusting the `tags` list's ordering, so the pin matches what the floating tag *actually* resolves to right now, not an assumption.
  - Notes: Did not re-push a test PR for this either — same reasoning as the prior entry, opening one is a visible action on the user's public repo and wasn't asked for here. Chose weekly Dependabot cadence (not daily) since this is a two-action, low-churn workflow — daily would just be more noise for the same eventual outcome.

- [x] STORY-006: Enable SQL Server data access for AI recommendations (REQ-007 + REQ-013) — closes R2/R3 PLANNED -> BUILT
  - Date: 2026-08-19
  - Session: CC-20260818-b7wq
  - What changed: Flagged two things before writing code, per this story's own "stop and ask" list. (1) No live SQL Server existed in this environment (`SQLSERVER_*` still empty) — same shape of gap as STORY-003's credential question, but not a full blocker: the acceptance criteria are structured around both outcomes (connected / unreachable), and "unreachable" was the actual, honest state of the environment at the time, so criterion 2 could be genuinely verified live from the start. User chose to close the gap for real rather than accept the caveat — walked through creating a real Azure SQL Database (Free tier) in the portal (their account, their browser; I only gave directions, never touched credentials — filled in `SQLSERVER_HOST`/`DATABASE` from a screenshot once deployed, left `USER`/`PASSWORD` for the user to enter directly). (2) A real design tension with existing code: `dmvReader.ts`'s established philosophy is silent fallback-to-fixture on any SQL failure, but this story's criterion 2 wants an explicit connectivity notification — generating an "AI-driven recommendation using SQL Server data" from fixture data and calling it real would be exactly the fabrication `rootCauseAgent.ts` was built to refuse. Resolved by having the new service bypass `dmvReader.ts`'s fallback entirely rather than reuse it. Built: (1) `mcp-server/src/recommendationService.ts` — `generateRecommendation()` calls `queryLiveDmv()` directly, zod-validates every row before treating it as evidence (new: extends the validate-before-trust discipline `rootCauseAgent.ts`/`diagnosticsGatherer.ts` already apply to Claude's responses, to the SQL layer too), reuses `analyzeIncidentRootCause()` as-is. Any SQL failure (unreachable, timeout, circuit-open — `queryLiveDmv()`'s existing reliability wrapper already distinguishes these) collapses to one `SqlServerUnavailableError`, covering "connection failure" and "data retrieval timeout" with infrastructure that already existed. A malformed row throws `InvalidDataFormatError`, giving that named failure path real, testable meaning instead of a checkbox. Caught and fixed a real gap in my own first draft before it shipped: the `InvalidDataFormatError` path wasn't wrapped in the same try/catch as the SQL-failure path, so it would have skipped logging — fixed to log both. (2) New `GET /api/recommendation` route, every attempt logged regardless of outcome. 5 new tests. (3) `mcp-server/src/seedBlockingScenario.ts` — demo/dev tooling (not part of the app) that deliberately opens two real concurrent SQL sessions, one holding a lock the other waits on, so a live demo has something incident-shaped to show instead of an idle database; the one place in this repo that writes to a monitored SQL Server, and deliberately so.
  - Verification, all against the real, live Azure SQL Database, not mocked: ran `queryLiveDmv()` directly — real connection succeeded, real columns matched `DmvExecRequestRow` exactly. Ran the full chain (`generateRecommendation()`) against the idle database — correctly returned `"No incident indicated"` at 10% confidence with `insufficientEvidence: true`, refusing to invent a problem on an empty system rather than fabricating one to look useful. Ran `seedBlockingScenario.ts` for real, then hit `/api/recommendation` while the lock was actively held — **the blocking scenario did not show up.** Investigated rather than assuming the seed script was broken: a raw, uncapped diagnostic query during the same live block confirmed the blocked session was real and present (`session_id: 53, blocking_session_id: 52, status: "suspended", cpu_time: 0`) but buried below ~50 Azure background system sessions, because the existing `DMV_QUERIES` (written before this session, R2/R3) ordered `TOP (3)` results by `cpu_time DESC` — a blocked session has near-zero CPU by definition, so it never ranked. A real, previously undiscoverable bug: fixture data's clean 3-row dataset could never have exposed it. Fixed in `dmvLiveSource.ts` (excludes `status = 'background'`, sorts any blocked session ahead of everything else), added a regression test asserting the query text, then re-ran the full live scenario end to end: `/api/recommendation` correctly returned `"Session 68's UPDATE on coreops-demo is blocked (LCK_M_S_XACT_MODIFY) by session 64, causing the order update to hang"` at 75% confidence — genuinely evidence-grounded reasoning over a real, live blocking scenario, not a template. Also caught and fixed a bug in `seedBlockingScenario.ts` itself along the way: Session B's `requestTimeout` (10s) was shorter than Session A's hold time (25s in that run), so Session B gave up waiting before ever observing the unblock — fixed to a longer, decoupled request timeout. `mcp-server` 62/62 tests passing (up from 56), `tsc --noEmit` clean; `guardrails`/`frontend` unaffected and re-confirmed clean. Full grep across all tracked files and `git rev-list --all | xargs git grep` for the real SQL password: zero matches, confirmed never leaked. Verified the Command Center renders STORY-006 correctly in Real mode (`"not_started"`, all 3 criteria confirmed) via a real browser check.
  - Notes: Confidence this story is complete and correct: ~92% — higher than STORY-003/004/005, because this is the first story where the specific thing that could have been fabricated (a "connected" scenario nobody could actually verify) got verified for real, not accepted as a caveat, and doing so surfaced and fixed a genuine pre-existing bug rather than just adding new code on top of an unproven foundation. `project-blueprint/requirements.md`: R2 and R3 moved PLANNED -> BUILT — both had been stuck specifically on "never run against a real SQL Server instance" since 2026-08-07/10, now closed. `docs/implementation-notes.md` fully refreshed (was stale — still said "R1 is UNMAPPED," old test counts) rather than just appended to. What would raise confidence further: running this against a non-serverless SQL Server (or one with less background noise) to confirm the fix generalizes beyond Azure's specific internal session mix, and a longer-running demo rehearsal to confirm the auto-pause cold-start delay (serverless tier, 1hr idle timeout) doesn't surprise anyone mid-demo.

- [x] STORY-008: Enhance monitoring capabilities for continuous incident management (REQ-016)
  - Date: 2026-08-19
  - Session: CC-20260819-x3mq
  - What changed: This story's acceptance criteria say "Given a server" generically (unlike STORY-006/007, which named their system explicitly), and I have no Windows Server credential or access. Flagged this before writing code rather than silently substituting something — user's direction: no faking, this needs to demo convincingly (explicitly: "viewers, recruiters need to know i can test this smoothly"). Resolved by reading "a server" as the one real, already-connected system this repo can actually reach — the live Azure SQL Database from STORY-006, explicitly listed as "already specified, reuse it, do not rebuild it" — rather than requesting new, uncredentialed Windows Server access for a generic phrase. User confirmed. Built in confirmed, paced steps: (1) `mcp-server/src/monitoringService.ts` — `startMonitoring()`/`stop()`, a real `setInterval` loop calling `queryLiveDmv()` directly (never `dmvReader.ts`'s fixture-fallback — a fabricated incident or a fabricated all-clear would be exactly the thing STORY-006/007 refused to do). Incident = any row with a non-zero `blocking_session_id`, reusing STORY-006's already-fixed, already-tested query as-is rather than writing new detection logic that could reintroduce that bug's class of error. A failed cycle logs the failure and the loop keeps running (this is what makes "continuous" real rather than aspirational — covers the "Monitoring failure" failure path); a logging failure is itself guarded so it can't kill the loop either (covers "Monitoring log failure"). Exposes an `onCycle` callback so a caller can observe live status without that being the audit trail itself (`logEvent` remains that). (2) Wired `GET/POST /api/monitoring/{start,stop,status}` into `httpServer.ts` so a demo can toggle monitoring live and see it running instead of tailing server logs — start/stop are idempotent (calling start while already running does not spin up a second interval, matching this repo's idempotency rule; same for stop).
  - Verification: 81/81 `mcp-server` tests passing (up from 74 before this story, including a regression test — see below), `tsc --noEmit` clean. Ran it live against the real Azure SQL Database, not mocked. First live run surfaced a real bug before it shipped: the interval fires on a fixed schedule regardless of whether the previous cycle finished, and the SQL Server free-tier instance was cold (auto-paused) and took ~30s to respond against the 10s default interval — overlapping cycles fired concurrently, piling up connection-attempt failures fast enough to trip the shared circuit breaker (from `dmvLiveSource.ts`) on a false alarm, surfacing as `CircuitOpenError` instead of the server actually being unreachable. Fixed with an in-flight guard in `monitoringService.ts` (skip a tick if the previous cycle hasn't finished) plus a new regression test asserting no overlapping `queryLiveDmv()` calls. Re-ran live after the fix: clean cycles every 10s exactly (`21:57:00`, `:10`, `:20`, `:30`), no circuit trips. Then ran `mcp-server/src/seedBlockingScenario.ts` (STORY-006's existing demo tool, reused not rebuilt) to create a real 15s blocking scenario while monitoring was live — the very next cycle correctly detected it (`incidentDetected: true`, session 79 blocked by session 77 on `coreops-demo`) and fired a real `incident_alert`, visible both in the server's log and via `GET /api/monitoring/status`. Confirmed `POST /api/monitoring/stop` is idempotent (second call returns `"Monitoring is already stopped."` rather than erroring) and that `status` correctly reflects `running: false` afterward. `.colaberry/progress.json` updated: all 3 STORY-008 criteria set to `passed: true`, `files_touched`/`tests_added`/`notes` filled in, validated as well-formed JSON.
  - Notes: Confidence this story is complete and correct: ~88% — every criterion was verified against real, live infrastructure end-to-end (continuous detection, a real incident triggering a real alert, and Trust logging), and the live run caught and fixed a genuine concurrency bug rather than shipping it. Slightly below STORY-006/007's ~90-92% because this story's "a server" required an interpretive call (SQL Server standing in for the persona's literal "Windows Server Administrator") rather than a literal, unambiguous match — flagged and confirmed with the user rather than silently assumed, but it's still a substitution worth being upfront about in any demo narration. What would raise it further: a longer-duration live soak (multiple minutes, several incident/clear cycles) to build more confidence the in-flight guard holds up beyond the two cycles observed here, and — if a real Windows Server ever becomes available — retargeting or extending this to monitor it directly, which would remove the interpretive gap entirely.

- [x] STORY-009: Implement incident escalation based on confidence thresholds (REQ-011)
  - Date: 2026-08-19

Confidence < 60 builds an `EscalationRecord` and logs a distinct `escalation_triggered` event (warn level, `notifiedOperator: true`) — this is this story's walking-skeleton meaning of "notify a human operator," the same pattern STORY-008 used for "alert": a real, distinct log signal, not a paging/email channel (explicitly STORY-010's job, not this one). One log line carries both the confidence score and an ISO timestamp, satisfying criteria 2 and 3 together. Confidence >= 60 returns `null` with no log call — tested explicitly at the exact 59/60 boundary so "doesn't over-escalate" is proven, not assumed (covers "Escalation not triggered"). A malformed confidence score (`NaN`, negative, >100, +-`Infinity`) throws a typed `InvalidConfidenceScoreError` rather than silently escalating or silently not (covers "Incorrect confidence score"). The log call is guarded the same way STORY-008's was, so a logging hiccup can't silently swallow an escalation or crash the caller (covers "Escalation log failure"). Wired into both existing recommendation paths — `GET /api/recommendation` (STORY-006, SQL) and `GET /api/cloud-recommendation` (STORY-007, cloud) — by calling `evaluateEscalation()` right after the AI result comes back and adding the result to the JSON response; the story's own "reuse, don't rebuild" list only named STORY-007/008, but REQ-011 doesn't scope by source, so both were included — flagged to the user before building, confirmed. Both of this system's real autonomous actions confirmed dispatching a real, logged notification end-to-end, live.

Deliberately a thin `{event, outcome, context}` adapter over `logEvent()`'s existing shape rather than a new rigid schema, since an operational event's shape genuinely varies per event type (unlike a decision or an ABAC check, which do have a fixed shape). Added `buildSystemEventAuditEntry()` (matching the existing builder pattern exactly: `id`, `correlationId`, injectable `now()`), a `freezeEntry()` case for `context`, and updated the header comment to record this second extension to the entry-type union. - [x] Reusable AI prompt library: 5 prompts, test-first, scored (no platform story)
  - Date: 2026-08-20
  - Session: CC-20260820-g4tk
  - What changed: User wanted a library of reusable prompts for CoreOps, non-technical, plain-English throughout. First identified and ranked 5 candidate prompts grounded in CoreOps's real pipeline (`score-root-cause-confidence`, `classify-incident-severity`, `draft-remediation-summary`, `draft-diagnostic-script`, `draft-operator-notification`), each a repeated task with a definite-shape answer, explicitly excluding a 6th candidate (remediation risk classification) since `architecture.md` deliberately keeps that decision out of the AI's hands. Built all 5 test-first: for each, `prompts/<name>/eval.jsonl` (5 cases each — 4 ordinary + 1 deliberately awkward) was written and every expected answer proposed one at a time and confirmed or corrected by the user directly, never filled in unilaterally. Built `scripts/score_prompt.py` once (shared across all 5): fills `{{blank}}` placeholders into a prompt file, calls Claude, extracts the first parseable JSON object from the reply, and grades only the fields named in `"expected"` (text case/whitespace-insensitive, numbers with a tolerance) — deliberately never grading open-ended fields like `rootCause`/`what_broke`/`script`/`message`, since exact-matching free text would fail correct answers for being phrased differently; those fields stay real, required prompt output, just ungraded by this script. Each prompt file (`v1.0.0.md`, `v1.0.1.md` for the one that needed a second pass) written via the harness's own Plan Mode: presented which of Monday's 5 prompt-engineering rungs to start at and why, got explicit approval, then wrote the file. All 5 started at Rung 2 (Specific) and only one — `classify-incident-severity` — needed to move up (a Rung 4 worked example, to fix one case about a worsening-but-unresolved problem correctly outranking a resolved one-off) after the score, not a guess, showed Rung 2 wasn't enough on that one case.
  - Verification: All 5 prompts scored against their real eval files via real Claude API calls (not simulated), each run at least twice before being marked `status: ready` in its own header, given the model's inherent sampling variance (see next note). Final scores: `classify-incident-severity` v1.0.1 — 3 clean runs of 5/5 and 1 run of 4/5, all 4 misses on the same subtle case; `score-root-cause-confidence` v1.0.0 — 5/5 twice; `draft-remediation-summary` v1.0.0 — 5/5 twice; `draft-diagnostic-script` v1.0.0 — 5/5 (after a real scorer bug fix, below); `draft-operator-notification` v1.0.0 — 5/5 twice.
  - Notes: Three real bugs found and fixed in `scripts/score_prompt.py` itself while building this, each from an actual failure, not anticipated in advance: (1) `claude-sonnet-5` rejects a `temperature` parameter outright ("deprecated for this model," confirmed by the API, not assumed) — removed the attempt to force determinism and added an explicit usage note that scores can vary run to run, so a close score difference needs 2-3 runs before trusting it as real. (2) `draft-diagnostic-script`'s replies (a full script wrapped in JSON) were sometimes cut off by `max_tokens=1024` before the JSON closed, which failed to parse and looked exactly like a wrong answer rather than a too-small limit — confirmed by inspecting a raw truncated response directly, not guessed; raised to `MAX_TOKENS = 4096` (a named constant) and added a `stop_reason == "max_tokens"` warning so a future truncation is diagnosable, not mysterious. (3) Any Claude API error beyond the two originally handled (bad/missing key, connection failure) was letting a raw Python stack trace through, contradicting the user's own "no stack traces, plain English instead" requirement even though they'd only named the key-missing case explicitly — added a general `anthropic.APIStatusError` handler that prints Claude's actual error message. No `.colaberry/plan.json` requirement covers a general prompt library, so no `Story:` trailer on this work — real, tested, and reused across the whole session, but outside this repo's scored surface by design, same as the governance-engine and audit-trail-design entries above.

- [x] STORY-011: implement rollback capabilities for low-risk tasks (REQ-015) — last story in the plan
  - Date: 2026-08-21
  - Session: CC-20260819-x3mq
  - What changed: No real Execution Service exists in this codebase — nothing performs a real production write, by design (only the read paths for SQL Server and Blob Storage exist). Flagged before writing code: "roll back a low-risk task" can't honestly mean "revert a real production change" when nothing real changes production yet; building a fake revert for an action nothing can actually execute would be exactly the fabrication this project has refused everywhere else. Proposed and confirmed with the user: target the one genuinely real, already-idempotent, low-risk reversible action already built — `monitoringService.ts`'s `startMonitoring()`/`stop()` (STORY-008). Built `mcp-server/src/rollbackService.ts` — `requestRollback(request, registry, options)`, checks run in a fixed order (permission -> task identification -> reversibility -> dependency) so a denial always names the *first* real reason, not a generic one; `registry` is a required parameter with no built-in default, since any default would either be empty (implying nothing is reversible) or would have to fabricate a revert function ahead of real wiring existing. Wired into `httpServer.ts`: `POST /api/monitoring/start` now returns a real `taskId`; a registry maps `start_monitoring` to a real revert closing over the actual `monitoringHandle` (calls its real `stop()`), and registers `notify_operators` as honestly non-reversible (`reason: "A delivered push notification cannot be unsent."`) rather than leaving it unrecognized, so its denial correctly reads "not reversible" and not "unknown task" — the right criterion for the right reason. New `POST /api/rollback` route maps all four `rollbackService` error types (insufficient permissions, unknown task type, not reversible, dependency blocked) to distinct HTTP statuses (403/404/409/409).
  - Verification: 6 new tests in `rollbackService.test.ts` — successful revert, each of the three named failure paths (insufficient permissions, incorrect task identification, dependency blocker) plus the REQ-015-specific non-reversible denial, and a Trust check that success and denial both log distinctly. `mcp-server` 108/108 tests passing (up from 102), `tsc --noEmit` clean. The test-writing process itself caught a real bug before it shipped: the non-reversible denial's human-readable explanation was keyed as `reason` in the log context, colliding with and silently overwriting the categorical denial code (also `reason`) on the same log line — renamed to `detail`, confirmed live afterward (see below) that both fields now appear correctly and distinctly. Verified live, not just mocked: started the real HTTP server, called `POST /api/monitoring/start` (got back a real `taskId` and confirmed `running: true` via status), called `POST /api/rollback` for that exact `taskId`/`start_monitoring` — got `{outcome: "reverted"}` back and confirmed via `GET /api/monitoring/status` that `running` actually flipped to `false` for real (the monitoring loop genuinely stopped, not just a claimed success). Then confirmed both denial paths live: `notify_operators` rollback → real `409 TASK_NOT_REVERSIBLE` with the honest reason text; an `engineer` role attempting rollback → real `403 INSUFFICIENT_PERMISSIONS`. Both denials appeared in the server's own audit log with correct, distinct `reason`/`detail` fields.
  - Notes: Confidence this story is complete and correct: ~90% — this is the highest-fidelity story in the release specifically because criterion 1 ("successfully reverts the task") was proven against a real, live state change (monitoring actually stopped), not a mocked assertion, matching the bar every other live-verified story in this session set. The one honest caveat, same shape as STORY-008's: "a low-risk task" was interpreted as monitoring start/stop rather than a literal production remediation, because no production-write capability exists yet to interpret it any other way — flagged and confirmed with the user before building, not discovered as a surprise later. This is the last story in `r4`'s plan; all 12 stories (STORY-000 through STORY-011) now have real, live-verified implementations in this repo.

- [x] Fix unescaped external data in `dashboard.html`'s `innerHTML` sinks (no platform story)
  - Date: 2026-08-21
  - Session: CC-20260819-x3mq
  - What changed: User asked whether CoreOps's trust boundaries are safe. Audited `dashboard.html` rather than answering from general impressions — grepped every `.innerHTML` assignment and traced where each interpolated value actually originates. Found the AI-generated root-cause text (the highest-risk single field, since it's LLM output) was already correctly rendered via `.textContent`, never `.innerHTML` — the boundary that matters most was already right. Found a real, concrete gap: several `.innerHTML` sinks concatenate values straight from `fetch()` responses without escaping — live DMV row fields (`status`, `wait_type`, `command`, `database_name`) in the session cards, and, most concretely, `POST /api/guardrail/decide`'s 404 error path, which echoes a client-supplied `itemId` verbatim into `resultEl.innerHTML` (`No pending remediation with id "${itemId}"` — and `itemId` comes straight from the request body). Not exploitable through the dashboard's own normal use today (it only ever sends IDs it generated itself), but the HTTP routes are public and would return whatever a direct caller sent, and this file already renders some of that via `innerHTML` — the kind of gap that's cheap to close now and expensive to find later. User said "fix."
  - What changed (the fix): Added one `escapeHtml()` helper (standard `&<>"'` entity escaping) near the top of the script, and wrapped every value reaching an `innerHTML` template that did not originate as a string literal in this file — DMV row fields, every `body.*` field from a `fetch()` response (`message`, `error`, `confidence`, `approver`, `taskId`, `violations[]`), and audit-trail entry fields (`entryType`, `event`/`hitlEventType`/`actionType`, `actor`). Applied uniformly rather than judging field-by-field which ones are "safe enough" right now — a field that's a fixed enum today can become free-form later, and re-litigating that judgment per field is exactly how this class of bug survives a refactor. Values already rendered via `.textContent` (root cause, most banners, most KPI values) were left untouched — `.textContent` is already safe and re-escaping it would be a no-op at best. The client-side command palette's `commands[]` array (developer-defined literals, never from a `fetch()` response) was also correctly left alone.
  - Verification: `tsc --noEmit` clean, `mcp-server` 118/118 tests unaffected (dashboard.html is static, no `.ts` touched). Live-verified the actual exploit path, not just the helper in isolation: called the real `decideRemediation('approve')` function against the live server with `currentGuardrailItemId` set to `<img src=x onerror=alert(1)>` — the server's real 404 response echoed that string back verbatim in `message` (confirmed via the raw response), and after rendering through the app's own (now-fixed) code path, `resultEl.querySelector('img')` returned `null` — the payload rendered as inert, visible text (`&lt;img src=x onerror=alert(1)&gt;`), not a live element; no `onerror` fired. Then confirmed legitimate content still renders cleanly with no double-escaping artifacts: a real `proposeRemediation()` call and a real Live Incidents reload both displayed normally (plain text, correct violation codes, correct session details), confirming the fix doesn't corrupt normal output.
  - Notes: No `.colaberry/plan.json` requirement covers this, so no `Story:` trailer. This was found and fixed proactively (a real audit, not a reported bug) — flagged to the user as part of a trust-boundary question rather than discovered by exploit. Scope was deliberately narrow: this fixes `dashboard.html`'s rendering layer only; it does not add server-side output encoding or a Content-Security-Policy header, since the concrete gap was specifically "untrusted data reaching the DOM unescaped," and CSP/server-side hardening would be new, larger scope not asked for here.

- [x] Session-based authentication for the CoreOps API and dashboard (no platform story)
  - Date: 2026-08-23
  - Session: CC-20260819-x3mq
  - What changed: A trust-boundary audit two entries above found the biggest real gap: every route on `httpServer.ts` was open to anyone who could reach the port, including `POST /api/guardrail/decide` — and `guardrails/hitlQueue.ts`'s real, enforced "only the assigned approver may decide" check was comparing a hardcoded constant (`GUARDRAIL_PRIMARY_APPROVER`) against itself, so it could never actually fail. User asked for real auth; decided session-based over JWT (no distributed system here for JWT's statelessness to help with, and a JWT in `localStorage` is directly readable by the exact XSS class of bug fixed two entries above). Entered Plan Mode given the size and security sensitivity of the change (multi-file, new routes, new UI flow, a genuine architectural decision) — explored the codebase (confirmed via a dedicated read-only pass: no existing auth code anywhere, the separate `frontend/` React app calls exactly one of the routes being gated, `index.ts`'s stdio MCP transport is a genuinely separate, non-network surface out of scope), had a Plan subagent produce a file-level implementation plan, verified its citations directly, then built it in the plan's own small-step order with `npm test`/`tsc --noEmit` and live verification after each step, per this repo's own "small, reversible steps" rule.
  - What changed (new modules, `mcp-server/src/auth/`): `credentials.ts` — `hashPassword`/`verifyPassword` via `node:crypto`'s `scryptSync`/`timingSafeEqual`, zero new dependencies. Hashed at rest despite `SQLSERVER_PASSWORD` being plaintext today — this password gates every route including the one that executes a real remediation, and hashing costs nothing; the inconsistency is noted in a comment rather than silently diverged from. `hashPassword.ts` — a `npm run hash-password -- '<password>'` CLI for first-time `.env` setup. `sessionStore.ts` — `SessionStore` class, same injectable-options shape as `guardrails/hitlQueue.ts`/`reliability/circuitBreaker.ts` (`ttlMs`, `now`, `generateId`), 60-minute sliding-expiry sessions (slides forward on each valid access so a long demo doesn't get logged out mid-session; lazily expires on access, same pattern as `CircuitBreaker.checkAvailability()`). `cookies.ts` — hand-rolled `Set-Cookie`/`Cookie` parsing (no `cookie` npm package needed for one cookie); `HttpOnly` + `SameSite=Strict` (does the real CSRF-mitigation work here, without a separate CSRF token) + a `Secure` flag gated by `SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE` (default off, so plain `http://localhost:8787` keeps working).
  - What changed (`httpServer.ts`): `AUTH_USERNAME`/`AUTH_PASSWORD_HASH` read at module load, **fail fast** if either is unset — a deliberate exception to this file's usual "missing config → fall back gracefully" convention, since falling back here would mean silently serving with no real auth. New routes: `GET /login` (serves a new `login.html`, `readFileSync`-at-startup, same pattern as `dashboardHtml`), `POST /api/login` (one generic `401 INVALID_CREDENTIALS` for either a wrong username or password — no enumeration), `POST /api/logout` (idempotent), `GET /api/session`. A `requireSession()` two-line guard added to every existing route except `GET /`, `GET /health`, `GET /login`, `POST /api/login`, `POST /api/logout` — `GET /` itself stays open (pure markup, no embedded data) since the dashboard's own `fetch` calls are the only mechanism that can also catch a session expiring *mid*-session, making a server-side redirect on `GET /` redundant extra code for no added coverage. **The actual fix for the original motivating gap:** `GUARDRAIL_PRIMARY_APPROVER` now derives from `AUTH_USERNAME` (the real, verified login identity) instead of `OPERATOR_CONTACTS` (never verified against anything, kept for its own separate job — the notification-recipient display name), and `POST /api/guardrail/decide` passes the real `session.username` as `decidedBy` to `hitlQueue.decide()` instead of the constant. `UnauthorizedDeciderError` is now reachable for real instead of permanently dead code.
  - What changed (`dashboard.html`): one shared `apiFetch()` wrapper (redirects to `/login` on any `401`) replacing all 11 existing raw `fetch()` calls — the vanilla-JS equivalent of an axios interceptor. The previously-decorative user-chip (hardcoded "QN"/"Quincy Ninying") is now real, populated from `GET /api/session` on load (which also doubles as the page's fast initial auth check), with a working logout button next to it.
  - What changed (`.claude/skills/demo-start/SKILL.md`, `demo-stop/SKILL.md`): both make real, already-tested `curl` calls against gated routes (`/api/monitoring/start`/`stop`) — added a login-and-cookie-jar step before any gated call in each. **Two real bugs caught by actually running the updated skills, not just writing them:** (1) `. .env` failed outright in this environment (zsh's `.`/`source` didn't find a bare filename in cwd — needed `./.env`); (2) even with the right path, blanket-sourcing `.env` as shell code broke on `OPERATOR_CONTACTS`'s unquoted value (`Quincy Ninying (AI Engineer)`) — zsh tried to glob-expand the parentheses. Fixed by extracting only the two needed values via `grep '^AUTH_USERNAME=' .env | cut -d= -f2-` instead of sourcing the whole file — safer in general too, since it doesn't execute arbitrary `.env` content as shell code. This needs a **plaintext** `AUTH_PASSWORD` in `.env` alongside the hash (the hash alone can't authenticate) — consistent with every other operational secret in this repo already living in plaintext `.env` (`SQLSERVER_PASSWORD`, `AZURE_STORAGE_CONNECTION_STRING`, `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`), not a new risk class.
  - Verification: 19 new unit tests (`credentials.test.ts`, `sessionStore.test.ts` — including injectable-clock boundary tests for sliding expiry, `cookies.test.ts`), each happy/failure/boundary. `mcp-server` 137/137 passing (up from 118), `guardrails` 50/50 unaffected, `tsc --noEmit` clean in both. Live-verified extensively against the real running server rather than trusting the wiring: wrong password → real `401`; correct login → real `Set-Cookie` (`HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Strict`, `Max-Age=3600`, no `Secure` locally as configured); every previously-open route confirmed `401`s without a session and works with one; the full guardrail propose→decide flow confirmed the `approver` field and the audit trail's `actor` field both now show `"quincy"` (the real login) instead of a hardcoded string; confirmed in a real browser via the actual login form that an unauthenticated visit to `/` redirects to `/login`, a successful login lands on the dashboard with the real username/initials shown, `document.cookie` cannot read the session token (`HttpOnly` genuinely blocks JS access — the concrete reason session cookies beat a JWT in `localStorage`), and logout genuinely destroys the server-side session (a subsequent visit to `/` redirects to `/login` again, not just a client-side navigation). Then ran `demo-start` and `demo-stop` for real, end to end, from a cold state — SQL Server itself is genuinely unavailable this month (Azure free-tier monthly quota exhausted, confirmed via the real error message, renews 2026-09-01 UTC — unrelated to this work, reported plainly rather than worked around, per the skill's own "don't claim readiness if a step didn't actually succeed" rule), but every auth-dependent step (server start, login, authenticated monitoring start/stop) was independently verified working, which is what this task needed to prove.
  - Notes: No `.colaberry/plan.json` requirement covers this, so no `Story:` trailer, matching every other governance/tooling entry this session. Explicitly not built (flagged, not silently left worse): `frontend/`'s React/Vite dev-proxy cookie/CORS handling (its own code already admits its deployment topology is undecided — gating `/api/dashboard/summary` means its one existing call will now `401` until that's addressed); real MFA (`mfa: true` stays an honest stand-in — no MFA exists anywhere in this system, only real login identity was added); multi-user credential storage (single `AUTH_USERNAME`/`AUTH_PASSWORD_HASH` pair, matching this repo's actual single-operator reality); login rate-limiting/lockout; the pre-existing unbounded `readJsonBody` size limit (separately flagged earlier, unrelated task). Rejected the alternative (`base: '/console/'`) specifically because it would create a fragile coupling — a `frontend/vite.config.ts` line that only makes sense in light of a decision made in a completely different file, easy to silently break if that config is touched later by someone with no visibility into `httpServer.ts`'s routing.
  - What changed (new module, `mcp-server/src/staticFiles.ts`): `resolveStaticFilePath(rootDir, requestedPath)` — real path-traversal protection (`candidate === root || candidate.startsWith(root + sep)`, not a bare `startsWith(root)`, which is the classic bug that wrongly accepts a sibling directory like `/tmp/dist-evil` against root `/tmp/dist`), plus an `.isFile()` check rejecting directory requests; returns `null` for anything unsafe or missing, never throws. `mimeTypeFor(filePath)` — a small fixed extension→MIME map with a safe `application/octet-stream` default for anything unmapped.   - What changed (`httpServer.ts`): two new routes added right after the existing `GET /login` block. `GET /console` checks `existsSync(frontend/dist/index.html)` **per request**, not cached at module load like `dashboardHtml`/`loginHtml` (those are committed source guaranteed to exist; `dist/` only exists if and after a separate build ran, and must never crash the server or affect `/`, `/login`, `/health`, or the API if it hasn't) — missing returns `503 CONSOLE_NOT_BUILT` with a clear message via the existing `sendJson()` pattern (`503`, not `404`, since the route genuinely exists and will work once built). `GET /assets/*` calls `resolveStaticFilePath()`/`mimeTypeFor()` and serves the bytes, or `404`s. New `mcp-server/src/httpMcpServer.ts` is the network-facing StreamableHTTP entry point ADR-001 always intended, running in **stateless mode** (fresh `McpServer`+transport per request) to satisfy ADR-001's own "any replica can answer any request" driver. Gated by a new `mcp-server/src/auth/apiToken.ts` (`verifyBearerToken()`, hand-rolled `timingSafeEqual` comparison) rather than the SDK's `requireBearerAuth()` — checked the actual installed SDK (`@modelcontextprotocol/sdk@1.30.0`) before building against it and found that function is an **Express** `RequestHandler` around an OAuth-shaped provider, while ADR-001's own original text referenced Python-SDK-style `AuthSettings`/`token_verifier` naming that doesn't exist in the TypeScript package actually installed here — corrected that in ADR-001's new Implementation addendum rather than silently building against the wrong API. `httpMcpServer.ts` fails fast at startup if `MCP_API_TOKEN` is unset, same deliberate exception to "degrade gracefully" as `AUTH_USERNAME`/`AUTH_PASSWORD_HASH`. Given the scope (new crypto primitive, login flow change, a real security surface), entered Plan Mode and dispatched a Plan subagent with full context to validate the design before writing any code — it verified every cited line/file directly against the real repo, confirmed the "login-gates-MFA collapses the need for session-level state" reasoning was sound while explicitly naming the real tradeoff being accepted (a stolen session cookie rides on one login's MFA for the full 60-minute TTL vs. a bounded per-decision re-entry), and caught a real gap I hadn't considered: `.claude/skills/demo-start/SKILL.md` and `demo-stop/SKILL.md` both `curl` the login route non-interactively and would silently break the moment a third required field landed. Spot-verified the agent's load-bearing citations (the `httpServer.ts` fail-fast block, the login handler, the `decide()` call site, both `SKILL.md` files' login blocks) directly before finalizing the plan.
  - What changed (`mcp-server/src/auth/totp.ts`, new): hand-rolled RFC 4648 base32 encode/decode (`node:crypto` has no base32 support), RFC 4226/6238 `generateTotpCode()` (HMAC-SHA1 + dynamic truncation), `buildOtpAuthUri()` (standard Key URI for manual authenticator-app entry — deliberately no QR code image generation, which would require a genuinely complex hand-rolled encoder or a new dependency for disproportionate benefit here), and `TotpVerifier` — a drift-tolerant (±1 step), replay-protected verifier (tracks `lastAcceptedTimestep`, rejects a reused code without permanently locking anything out). `generateTotpSecret.ts` (one-time setup CLI, mirrors `hashPassword.ts` exactly) and `printCurrentTotpCode.ts` (non-interactive current-code CLI, needed specifically to keep the two demo skills working) round out the new auth modules.
  - What changed (`httpServer.ts`): fail-fast guard extended to also require `MFA_TOTP_SECRET` (same deliberate exception to "degrade gracefully" as the existing auth config); `POST /api/login` now requires a third `totpCode` field, folded into the same single, non-distinguishing `401 INVALID_CREDENTIALS` check as username/password (short-circuit evaluation means a wrong password never reaches `totpVerifier.verify()`, so a mistyped password can't burn/replay-block the operator's real code). `hitlQueue.decide(..., true)` needed **no code change** — only its truthfulness changed, since every session past `requireSession()` is now inherently MFA-verified by construction (login itself requires TOTP); updated the comment directly above the call site to explain why, rather than leaving stale "this is a placeholder" language in place. `login.html` gained one more field, styled with the page's existing generic `label`/`input` CSS, `autocomplete="one-time-code"`. `.env.example` documents `MFA_TOTP_SECRET` in the same voice as the existing auth vars. Both demo `SKILL.md` files updated to generate a fresh code via the new CLI immediately before their existing login `curl`.
  - Verification: `totp.test.ts` (17 new tests) verified against the **published RFC 6238 Appendix B SHA1 test vectors** (exact expected 8-digit codes at 5 known Unix timestamps) — caught one real bug during this step, a transcription typo in my own test's hardcoded RFC 4648 base32 example (`MZXW6XTBOI======` vs. the actual `MZXW6YTBOI======`); all 5 real RFC 6238 TOTP vectors passed on the first run, isolating the bug to the test's own wrong expected string rather than the implementation, confirmed by fixing the string and re-running clean. `mcp-server` 184/184 (up from 167), `guardrails` 57/57 unaffected, `tsc --noEmit` clean in both. Live-verified extensively against the real running server: generated a real secret via the new CLI, restarted the server (fail-fast check passed with all three vars set), confirmed missing `totpCode` → real `400`; wrong code → real generic `401` (no field-specific leak); the real current code → real `200` + session cookie; **immediately replaying the exact same request → real rejection**, proving replay protection works against the live server, not just in isolation; the full `POST /api/guardrail/propose` → `POST /api/guardrail/decide` flow still succeeded afterward with the MFA-verified session, confirming `MfaRequiredError`/`"rejected_no_mfa"` is not hit for a legitimate call; a real login through the actual browser UI (new field rendered correctly, landed on the real dashboard); and the updated `demo-start` skill's exact login sequence succeeding non-interactively via the new `current-totp-code` CLI.
  - Notes: No `.colaberry/plan.json` requirement covers this, so no `Story:` trailer. `docs/ADR-006-totp-mfa.md` documents the mechanism decision and the session-level-MFA design collapse, including the tradeoff being accepted, matching ADR-003's own precedent of stating a limitation plainly rather than glossing over it. Explicitly not built (flagged, not silently skipped): QR code image generation, an in-app enrollment flow, per-decision TOTP re-verification, and multi-user secret storage — all named in ADR-006's "what would change this decision."

- [x] Build a real, open-source, live-pattern verification against Apache Superset (no platform story)
  - Date: 2026-08-24
  - Session: CC-20260819-x3mq
  - What changed: User asked for a lighter, open-source way to test the reporting-service side "so that we are honest about reporting services" — SSRS itself has no Linux/Docker distribution, so no fully-authentic lightweight path exists. Recommended and built a real, live verification against Apache Superset (genuinely open source, Docker-composable) instead — explicit and repeated throughout the deliverable that this proves the live-query-then-fallback *pattern* works against a real running system, not that it validates SSRS's actual `ExecutionLog3` schema/query. Built `mcp-server/dev-superset/`: `docker-compose.yml` (Superset + a small Postgres container, since Superset explicitly blocks SQLite as a SQL Lab source "for security reasons"), `setup.sh` (fully automates DB migration, admin creation, `superset init`, driver installation, database registration, and running one real successful + one real failing query), `verify-live-pattern.ts` (authenticates against Superset's real REST API, reads real query-execution history, structured exactly like `readDmv()`/`readSsrsExecutionLog()` — live-first, fallback-tagged on a known connection failure), and `README.md` stating plainly what this does and does not prove.
  - What changed (real problems hit and fixed while building this, not glossed over): (1) Superset's base image ships with zero SQL Lab database drivers installed, and even attempting to use its own built-in SQLite is explicitly rejected by Superset itself as a security risk — required adding a real Postgres container as the actual query target. (2) `pip install psycopg2-binary` — even run as root — silently installed to a `--user` site-packages path the venv's own Python never consults; looked successful (`Successfully installed`) but left the module unimportable until reinstalled with an explicit `--target` into the venv's real site-packages, and even then required a container restart to pick up. (3) Superset's CSRF protection needs a session cookie carried alongside the CSRF token header, not just the token in isolation — an early attempt failed with "CSRF session token is missing" until a cookie jar was added to every subsequent call. (4) `verify-live-pattern.ts`'s own first version had a real bug, caught only by actually stopping the Superset container and re-running: a connection-level failure (`ECONNREFUSED`) throws Node's raw `fetch` `TypeError` before any HTTP response exists, which the script's original error handling didn't catch — it crashed uncaught instead of falling back, exactly the class of gap this whole exercise exists to catch. Fixed by wrapping every fetch call in a translator that turns any network-level failure into the same `LiveSourceUnavailableError` an HTTP-level failure would produce. All four fixes are documented directly in `setup.sh`'s and `verify-live-pattern.ts`'s own comments, not just here.
  - Verification: Tore down every container and volume completely (`docker compose down -v`) and re-ran `setup.sh` from a genuinely clean slate, unattended, to confirm it's actually reproducible — not just something that worked because of manual troubleshooting along the way. It completed cleanly end to end. Ran `verify-live-pattern.ts` against the fresh instance: real `"source": "live"` with a genuine `status: "success"` row and a genuine `status: "failed"` row carrying a real Postgres error message and real timestamps. Stopped the Superset container mid-session and re-ran the script: correctly fell back with `"source": "fallback"` and a clear reason string, proving the fallback path against a real failure, not just a mocked one — this is exactly what caught bug (4) above. Restarted Superset and confirmed the live path recovered cleanly. `mcp-server`'s main `tsc --noEmit` and full test suite (205/205) confirmed unaffected by the new files. Tore everything down again (`docker compose down -v`) after verification, per this session's standing practice of not leaving dev infrastructure running.
  - Notes: No `.colaberry/plan.json` requirement covers this, so no `Story:` trailer. `mcp-server/dev-superset/` is explicitly not part of the shipped MCP tool surface and is not wired into `ssrsReader.ts`/`ssrsLiveSource.ts` in any way — those still target real SSRS's `ExecutionLog3` schema unchanged. This is verification tooling only, documented as such in its own README so it can't later be mistaken for "SSRS was tested live."

- [x] Close the audit-log rotation gap — ADR-005 addendum (no platform story)
  - Date: 2026-08-24
  - Session: CC-20260819-x3mq
  - What changed: User asked "no gaps anymore right?" — honest answer was no. Non-repudiation was still stuck at Band 3, tied to the one gap `docs/ADR-005-audit-trail-persistence.md` explicitly flagged as unsolved when it shipped: "No log rotation or compaction. The file grows without bound." User asked to tackle it next. Read `guardrails/auditLog.ts` and its test file in full before designing anything, to match the existing append-only, idempotent-by-id, opt-in-persistence conventions exactly rather than introducing a new persistence idiom.
  - What changed (`guardrails/auditLog.ts`): size-based rotation into numbered archive segments (`audit-log.1.jsonl`, `audit-log.2.jsonl`, ...) — deliberately **never deletion**: this is the governance record the system's "every production change has a verifiable human approval" claim rests on, so every entry stays queryable forever, just split across bounded files instead of one unbounded one. New optional `maxLinesPerSegment` constructor option (default 5,000 — comfortably above this system's actual current volume, small enough that any one archived file stays easy to open directly). `record()` now checks the active file's line count before each append and rotates first if the threshold is reached; rehydration now replays every archived segment (oldest first, by index) and then the active file into the same in-memory `Map`, so `retrieve()`/`all()`/`forCorrelationId()` are completely unchanged and don't know or care how many files the history spans. No separate index file or persisted counter — the next archive index is always derived by scanning the directory, cheap at this volume.
  - Verification: 7 new unit tests in `auditLog.test.ts` (rotates at threshold, does not rotate prematurely, rehydrates from archived + active segments together, rotates correctly across multiple restarts producing sequentially numbered archives, keeps every entry queryable in-memory across a rotation within one running instance, an idempotent duplicate right at a rotation boundary does not rotate or append again, the default threshold is high enough that ordinary use never rotates). `tsc --noEmit` clean in both `guardrails` and `mcp-server`. Full suite: `mcp-server` 214/214 (unaffected — `httpServer.ts`'s wiring didn't change, still uses the new default), `guardrails` 64/64 (up from 57), `frontend` 6/6 — **284 total**. **Live-verified against a copy of the real, running system's actual audit data, not synthetic data**: copied the real `mcp-server/data/audit-log.jsonl` (48 real entries at the time) to a scratch location, rehydrated it through the new code with a deliberately small threshold, recorded one more entry to force an immediate rotation, and confirmed the archived segment held all 48 original real lines byte-for-byte intact, the new entry landed in a fresh active file, and a second rehydration correctly saw all 49 entries. The real, live `mcp-server/data/audit-log.jsonl` itself was never touched by this test (48 lines, well under the real 5,000-line default) — only the scratch copy was, and it was deleted afterward along with the throwaway verification script.
  - Notes: No `.colaberry/plan.json` requirement covers this, so no `Story:` trailer. Updated `docs/ADR-005-audit-trail-persistence.md` in place with an "Implementation addendum" section (same pattern as ADR-001's earlier in-place update — finishing the same decision, not a different one) rather than opening a new ADR-008: documents the decision (size-based over time-based rotation, and why deletion was rejected outright for a governance record), the options considered, and the verification. Explicitly still not built (flagged, not silently skipped): no bound on in-memory growth (the `Map` still holds every entry for the life of the process — out of scope; this fix targets the flagged gap, file-size growth, not memory, and the system's real volume doesn't make memory growth a live concern yet), and no cross-process/shared-store rotation coordination (still single-process, single-file-per-segment, same limitation ADR-005 itself already named as a reason to move to a shared store if `mcp-server` is ever horizontally scaled).
